I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.

I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.

It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. It’s hard to compare, because memory edits things strangely, especially when you’re tired.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like the studio lights are on and there’s just no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that sounds dramatic, I know—people always think dancers talk in metaphors because we’re used to making shapes out of feelings—but it’s not just poetic. It’s how it feels. Just… empty and sad in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding it hard to enjoy dancing or other things you usually like?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.

Like I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.

Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.

pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would disappear.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’d sit down to finish a grant report and suddenly the sound of a truck outside, a photo on the wall, or one phrase in an old rehearsal note would pull me into a completely different plan.\n- key: S040\n  name: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.

I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like the studio lights are on and there’s just no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that sounds dramatic, I know—people always think dancers talk in metaphors because we’re used to making shapes out of feelings—but it’s not just poetic. It’s how it feels. Just… empty and sad in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding it hard to enjoy dancing or other things you usually like?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: My sleep has been all over the place—some nights I stayed up sketching formations until description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: I feel wiped out after small things, like answering one email to a dancer or showering with this ankle brace on, and lying down doesn’t really recharge me.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.

But the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.

So… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like the studio lights are on and there’s just no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that sounds dramatic, I know—people always think dancers talk in metaphors because we’re used to making shapes out of feelings—but it’s not just poetic. It’s how it feels. Just… empty and sad in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding it hard to enjoy dancing or other things you usually like?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. It’s hard to compare, because memory edits things strangely, especially when you’re tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there was another full two-week stretch before this where it was nearly every day. What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… anxious like before a performance? Because that’s such a specific animal—your whole body buzzing, counting beats in your teeth almost.\n\nLately it’s more like I’m sad and worn down, and sometimes I’m restless, but I don’t know if I’d say anxious a lot. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.

Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. It’s hard to compare, because memory edits things strangely, especially when you’re tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there was another full two-week stretch before this where it was nearly every day. What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… anxious like before a performance? Because that’s such a specific animal—your whole body buzzing, counting beats in your teeth almost.\n\nLately it’s more like I’m sad and worn down, and sometimes I’m restless, but I don’t know if I’d say anxious a lot. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? So then I reread them too much.\n\nBut whether that’s been most days for nine months… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… anxious like before a performance? Because that’s such a specific animal—your whole body buzzing, counting beats in your teeth almost.\n\nLately it’s more like I’m sad and worn down, and sometimes I’m restless, but I don’t know if I’d say anxious a lot. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? So then I reread them too much.\n\nBut whether that’s been most days for nine months… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing that really brought me in is what’s happening now. Waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this stretch… I don’t know, I keep trying to compare it to older times, but it gets blurry. Like, dancers are always tired, always disappointed about something, always acting like pain is just part of the job. So I can’t honestly say there was another clear two-week period like this. What I know for sure is this current sadness has been… different. More there when I wake up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. More there when I wake up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these past nine months, have you felt keyed up or unable to relax on most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Keyed up… I don’t know. I mean, I have trouble relaxing sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “most days.”

It’s hard because in dance you’re sort of trained to hold tension everywhere, even when you’re supposed to look effortless. Like your shoulders are down but your brain is doing eight counts ahead, and then someone says “just soften,” which is never helpful—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

I guess I haven’t been paying attention in that exact way. I couldn’t say for sure if it’s been most days.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.9, F41.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like the studio lights are on and there’s just no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that sounds dramatic, I know—people always think dancers talk in metaphors because we’re used to making shapes out of feelings—but it’s not just poetic. It’s how it feels. Just… empty and sad in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding it hard to enjoy dancing or other things you usually like?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. It’s hard to compare, because memory edits things strangely, especially when you’re tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there was another full two-week stretch before this where it was nearly every day. What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… anxious like before a performance? Because that’s such a specific animal—your whole body buzzing, counting beats in your teeth almost.\n\nLately it’s more like I’m sad and worn down, and sometimes I’m restless, but I don’t know if I’d say anxious a lot. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? So then I reread them too much.\n\nBut whether that’s been most days for nine months… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing that really brought me in is what’s happening now. Waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this stretch… I don’t know, I keep trying to compare it to older times, but it gets blurry. More there when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt keyed up or unable to relax on most days?\n\nPatient: Keyed up… I don’t know. I think you asked that a second ago, or maybe I answered it sideways. Before these nine months… I really can’t pin down a separate time that was clearly “nearly every day for two weeks.”

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like the studio lights are on and there’s just no music in me anymore. And I’m a dancer, so that sounds dramatic, I know—people always think dancers talk in metaphors because we’re used to making shapes out of feelings—but it’s not just poetic. It’s how it feels. Just… empty and sad in the morning.\n\nDoctor: Have you been finding it hard to enjoy dancing or other things you usually like?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say that exactly. Dancing is complicated, because it’s work, and it’s also like breathing, and then sometimes it’s like homework you gave yourself years ago and forgot you agreed to. I still show up, mostly. I mean, I can’t always tell if I’m enjoying it or just doing the steps because my body knows where to go.\n\nOther things… I haven’t really been paying attention, honestly. I’ll start thinking about it and then I’m thinking about rehearsal schedules or whether the floor at the studio needs refinishing, which sounds stupid, but a bad floor can ruin your knees. So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just know I don’t feel very lit up lately.\n\nDoctor: When this sadness is there, does it last for most of the day?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I wake up, definitely. That’s the part that scares me a little—the morning feels so… hollow. Like I open my eyes and the whole room is already dim, even if the sun’s coming in.\n\nMost of the day… I mean, it kind of follows me around, but I can get distracted. Rehearsal will start and I’ll be counting steps or watching someone’s alignment, and then suddenly I remember it again, like the music dropped out. So yes, I think it’s there a lot, but mornings are when I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this sadness?\n\nPatient: I think… about nine months? Around that. It’s hard to pin down because it didn’t arrive like some dramatic curtain drop. It was more like the lighting changed slowly and I kept thinking, well, maybe I’m tired, maybe it’s the season, maybe the studio schedule is just ridiculous.\n\nBut yeah, if I’m being honest, the depressed part has been there for about nine months.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful event or major change in your life around the time this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to find one, because it would be cleaner, wouldn’t it? Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. It’s hard to compare, because memory edits things strangely, especially when you’re tired.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there was another full two-week stretch before this where it was nearly every day. What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… anxious like before a performance? Because that’s such a specific animal—your whole body buzzing, counting beats in your teeth almost.\n\nLately it’s more like I’m sad and worn down, and sometimes I’m restless, but I don’t know if I’d say anxious a lot. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? So then I reread them too much.\n\nBut whether that’s been most days for nine months… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing that really brought me in is what’s happening now. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? So then I reread them too much.\n\nBut whether that’s been most days for nine months… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing that really brought me in is what’s happening now. Waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this stretch… I don’t know, I keep trying to compare it to older times, but it gets blurry. Like, dancers are always tired, always disappointed about something, always acting like pain is just part of the job. So I can’t honestly say there was another clear two-week period like this. What I know for sure is this current sadness has been… different. More there when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt keyed up or unable to relax on most days?\n\nPatient: Keyed up… I don’t know. I mean, I have trouble relaxing sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “most days.”\n\nIt’s hard because in dance you’re sort of trained to hold tension everywhere, even when you’re supposed to look effortless. Like, “oh, that happened, and then I got sad.” But I don’t know. Nothing obvious jumps out.\n\nThere were normal stresses—there are always stresses, especially with dance, funding, schedules, people deciding at the last second that they can’t make rehearsal because their cat has an appointment or whatever. And then I get stuck thinking about how strange it is that a room full of adults can all agree to move together at the same time and still nobody answers emails.\n\nBut a major change? I’m not sure. It didn’t feel like one big thing. It was more like I looked up and realized I’d been feeling this way for a while.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, had you already been feeling down more often than not for at least two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was two years. I mean, I’ve had low patches before—everyone in dance has these strange bleak weeks where your body hurts and the mirrors feel personally insulting—but this… this depressed part I’m talking about has really been about nine months.\n\nBefore that, I don’t think I was down more often than not for years. It’s more like it settled in during this past stretch.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure if I’d call anything before this exactly the same. What’s bringing me in now is this waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nI’ve had bad stretches, yes, but this one has a particular… emptiness to it. What I can really point to is now—this thing that brings me in, waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this, I might’ve had sad days, but I’m not sure I’d call it the same.\n\nDoctor: Along with the sadness, have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less pleasure from things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not totally sure. Pleasure is a funny word, especially with dance, because sometimes you don’t enjoy it exactly—you just know where your body is supposed to go, and then afterward you realize it meant something.\n\nLately I haven’t really been thinking of things in terms of enjoying or not enjoying them. I’ll put music on and then get distracted by, like, whether the neighbor upstairs is dragging a chair or if it’s part of the rhythm. So… I don’t know. Maybe? I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling slowed down in your body or movements lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s strange because I’m used to my body being the one thing that knows what to do, even when my head doesn’t.\n\nBut lately, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down—like I’m moving through wet sand instead of music. And then I’ll notice the floorboards, or the dust by the mirror, and I sort of lose the thread of why I was walking over there. So… yes, slowed down, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been kind of slippery.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to finish a grant report—something I really do need to finish—and then a truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking of a whole different piece, or a lighting idea, or who should stand where, and then twenty minutes are gone.\n\nIt doesn’t always feel like “I can’t concentrate” exactly. It feels like everything keeps tugging at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more or less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… or just messier. My sleep’s been all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’m up way too late—like until it’s basically morning—sketching formations, moving people around on the page like little dots. And then other nights I do fall asleep because I’m so tired, but I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight, and then I’m just… awake. It doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. That’s actually kind of what made me come in.\n\nI keep waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like… like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic—I’m a choreographer, everything turns into an image—but it really feels that way. I’ll be lying there thinking about the day, or the studio, or just the coffee cup on the counter, and there’s this emptiness sitting there before anything has even happened.\n\nSo… yes. Most days. Nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Before this nine-month period, was there ever a time when you felt like this same kind of depression nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had low stretches before, like after a project ended or when I wasn’t getting cast in things, and everyone acts like dancers are supposed to just absorb disappointment like it’s part of the floorboards.\n\nBut the “nearly every day for two weeks” part—honestly, I don’t know how to measure it. I didn’t really keep track. Sometimes I think I was just tired, or lonely, or being dramatic about the work, and then I’d get distracted by a rehearsal schedule or some little costume problem and forget to ask whether it was depression.\n\nSo… I can’t say clearly. Maybe? I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: In the past nine months, have you been feeling worried or nervous on most days?\n\nPatient: Worried or nervous… I don’t know. I mean, I get tense, but “most days” is hard to answer.\n\nSome days I’m worried about normal things, like studio space or whether someone understood the count changes, or if I sent an email that sounded too sharp. Actually, emails are terrible because there’s no body language, and dancers are all body language, you know? I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Before this current nine-month period, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I mean… the thing that really brought me in is what’s happening now. Waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness, like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore.\n\nBefore this stretch… I don’t know, I keep trying to compare it to older times, but it gets blurry. Like, dancers are always tired, always disappointed about something, always acting like pain is just part of the job. So I can’t honestly say there was another clear two-week period like this. What I know for sure is this current sadness has been… different. More there when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: During these past nine months, have you felt keyed up or unable to relax on most days?\n\nPatient: Keyed up… I don’t know. I mean, I have trouble relaxing sometimes, but I’m not sure if it’s “most days.”\n\nIt’s hard because in dance you’re sort of trained to hold tension everywhere, even when you’re supposed to look effortless. Like your shoulders are down but your brain is doing eight counts ahead, and then someone says “just soften,” which is never helpful—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t been paying attention in that exact way. I couldn’t say for sure if it’s been most days.\n\nDoctor: Before these past nine months, was there ever a separate period in your life when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think you asked that a second ago, or maybe I answered it sideways. Before these nine months… I really can’t pin down a separate time that was clearly “nearly every day for two weeks.”\n\nThere were hard patches, sure, like injuries or shows ending and you suddenly don’t know what to do with your body at 10 a.m. because rehearsal used to be your whole day.
